> hey man, today we will be making a "conversion landing page" for comparing    
  NYSE-traded ETFs for Czech retil investors who arrive from mobile ad, here is 
  the entire description of what i got "                                        
  A conversion landing page                                                     
                                                                                
  The goal is a landing page comparing NYSE-traded ETFs for a Czech retail      
  investor who arrives from an ad on mobile and spends only a few seconds on    
  the page. The page should work as a lead magnet: offer the visitor something  
  of value and get their contact details in exchange. A nice design isn't       
  enough. The page must be optimized for collecting contacts. It also must not  
  look like default Claude output. Both the design and the copy should be       
  thought through for this target group. My main question when evaluating it    
  will be: could we start running ads to it tomorrow?                           
                                                                                
  Define the target group, what the visitor gets in exchange for their contact  
  details, and at what moment you ask for them. The value should be obvious     
  before the visitor fills in the form. Also design two versions of the ad. The 
  first screen of the page should follow on from what the ad promised and       
  immediately communicate what the visitor will get.                            
                                                                                
  I leave the choice of interactive elements up to you, for example a fund      
  comparison, a fee calculator or a portfolio builder. Each of them should lead 
  to the contact form. The form should have as few fields as possible. Also     
  think about what the visitor gets after submitting it.                        
                                                                                
  Use real data with the source cited, and track the whole journey from arrival 
  to form submission. In the README, explain:                                   
  - why the sections are in the order you chose                                 
  - the conversion rate you expect                                              
  - three A/B test hypotheses, ranked by expected impact                        
                                                                                
  Deploy the page on free hosting, for example Vercel.

> i am sorry i should have told you before i would like to see tha plan first   

● No problem. I've stopped building, and nothing has been created in your
  folder. So far I've only checked live data so the plan uses real numbers.
  Here's the plan.

  The idea behind it

  The brief asks to compare NYSE ETFs (VOO, SPY, VTI…) for Czech retail
  investors. A Czech retail investor can't buy most of them. Because of the EU
  PRIIPs rules, US ETFs have no Czech KID document, so EU brokers block them for
  retail clients. Every Czech beginner who copies a US YouTuber runs into this.
  So the page doesn't only compare NYSE ETFs. It also shows each one's European
  "twin" (an Irish UCITS fund on the same index, e.g. VOO → VUAA) that they can
  actually buy. That's real value they can't easily find elsewhere, and it
  makes a strong hook.

  Working brand: "ETF tahák" (tahák = Czech for "cheat sheet"). It's a made-up
  brand, not a real company.

  Target group

  - Czech, 25–40, on mobile, earns above average.
  - Has savings sitting in a bank account or an expensive bank fund (about
    1.5–2.5% a year in fees).
  - Knows "S&P 500" from social media, but doesn't know which ETF to pick,
    worries about fees and taxes, and some have already been blocked when trying
    to buy VOO.
  - Distrusts Czech financial advisors who push products. That's why the copy
    says outright "nebudeme vám volat" ("no advisor will call you").

  What they get for their email

  A PDF "ETF tahák 2026":
  - 12 NYSE ETFs with fees, 10-year returns and yield.
  - Each one's buyable UCITS twin with its ISIN.
  - A one-page Czech tax summary: W-8BEN, the 3-year holding rule, the 100k CZK
    yearly exemption, and DIP (the Czech long-term investment account).

  They also get their own calculator result.

  When we ask for the email

  - Only one field: email.
  - We ask right after the visitor sees their personal loss number ("na
    poplatcích ztratíte 412 000 Kč", i.e. "you'll lose 412,000 CZK on fees"),
    when they care most.
  - The same form also sits in the hero for people who already decided from the
    ad, and under the locked table rows.
  - A sticky bottom button appears on mobile.

  Two ads, and a first screen that matches each one

  ┌─────────┬───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │         │          Ad A: fees           │          Ad B: twins          │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │         │ "Váš bankovní fond si bere 2  │ "Chtěli jste koupit VOO a     │
  │ Hook    │ %, VOO 0,03 %. Spočítejte si  │ broker řekl ne? Tady jsou     │
  │         │ rozdíl za 10 vteřin."         │ jeho evropská dvojčata."      │
  ├─────────┼───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ First   │ Calculator in the hero →      │ Cheat-sheet preview + first   │
  │ screen  │ result → email                │ table rows → email            │
  └─────────┴───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The utm_content parameter decides which hero shows. One page, with the section
  order switched by variant and no flicker.

  Page sections (fees variant)

  1. Hero with calculator
  2. What's in the cheat sheet
  3. ETF comparison table (2 twins visible, the rest locked)
  4. Three objections ("why can't I buy VOO?", "what's the catch?", "will you
     call me?")
  5. Final form
  6. Sources

  The README will explain the reasoning behind this order.

  After they submit

  - The table unlocks right away, and the PDF downloads instantly. They don't
    have to wait for the email.
  - The PDF is also sent by email.
  - One tap-to-answer question for lead quality ("Kolik měsíčně investujete?",
    i.e. "How much do you invest monthly?"), asked only after the form is sent
    so it doesn't cost conversions.
  - A WhatsApp share button.

  Data (real, with sources and date)

  - Prices and returns from the Yahoo Finance API, through a script you can
    re-run (scripts/fetch-data.mjs).
  - I've confirmed all 12 are listed on NYSE Arca.
  - Expense ratios are current. Some differ from what you might remember: VTI
    now tracks a Morningstar index, SPLG is now SPYM, and VGT has split.
  - Twin ISINs and fees checked on justETF and the issuers' sites.
  - Bank fund fees from ESMA's EU cost report.

  Tracking (whole journey)

  - Every step is tracked: visit with UTM and variant, scroll depth, each
    section seen, calculator use, table filter use, clicks on locked rows, every
    CTA, starting the form, form errors, form sent, PDF downloaded, the
    follow-up answer, and time to sign-up.
  - Our own cookieless tracking runs from the first click, so it needs no
    consent banner.
  - GA4, Meta Pixel (Lead event) and PostHog are ready to switch on by adding
    their IDs, and load only after consent.
  - Leads go to a Vercel serverless function, which saves them to Upstash Redis
    (free) and/or forwards them to a webhook. The email itself goes out through
    Resend. Each is switched on with an environment variable.

  Tech and design

  - Plain HTML, CSS and JS with no framework, for fast loading on mobile.
  - Look: "paper cheat sheet", with paper background, black ink, yellow
    highlighter, monospace ticker numbers and a few handwritten notes.
    Deliberately not the default gradient-and-cards style.
  - Ads are made as HTML and rendered to PNG in two formats (4:5 feed and 9:16
    story) using headless Chrome. The PDF is made the same way.

  README (in English)

  Covers:
  - Target group, offer, and when we ask for the email
  - The ads
  - The section order
  - Expected conversion rate: about 8–12% of visitors on cold mobile Meta
    traffic, with the funnel math and cost per lead
  - Three A/B tests ranked by impact: offer framing > when we ask > "nebudeme
    volat" wording
  - Tracking plan
  - Launch checklist answering "could we run ads tomorrow?"

  What I need from you

  1. gh and vercel aren't installed. Once the code is done I can install them
     (winget install GitHub.cli, npm i -g vercel), and you'd then log in with ! 
     gh auth login and ! vercel login. Or you can create the repo and import it
     in Vercel yourself.
  2. Repo name: I'd suggest etf-tahak-landing.
  3. Are the brand "ETF tahák", Czech page copy and an English README OK?

  Shall I go ahead?

● The page is built, tested and committed locally. Pushing it to GitHub and 
  deploying to Vercel are blocked until you log in to the two tools I just
  installed. Both logins open a browser, so they need you:

  ! "/c/Program Files/GitHub CLI/gh.exe" auth login --web -h github.com -p https
  ! vercel login

  If the first one hangs at a prompt, run gh auth login in your own terminal
  instead. Once you're logged in, I'll create the public repo etf-tahak-landing,
  push, deploy to Vercel, and put the live URL into the README.

  Where the plan ended up: it's in the repo, so it travels with the work.
  - docs/PLAN.md is the plan as you approved it, plus a table of what changed
    during the build and why.
  - README.md is the full strategy write-up: target group, the offer, when we
    ask for the email, both ads, why the sections are in that order, expected
    conversion rate, the 3 A/B tests ranked, and a launch checklist for "could
    we run ads tomorrow?".

  What's built:
  - Page (in Czech): two versions picked by the ad link with no flicker.
    - Ad A ("fees") opens on the calculator, already showing −466 000 Kč without
      a tap.
    - Ad B ("twins") opens on VOO→VUAA, SPY→SPYL, VT→VWCE plus the email form.
  - Comparison: 12 ETFs, all checked to be listed on NYSE Arca, using live data
    as of 8. 10. 2026. It shows 6 at first, and 9 of the 12 European twins are
    blurred until the visitor leaves an email.
  - The cheat sheet: a real 2-page PDF with the twins' ISINs and a page on Czech
    taxes. It downloads instantly after sign-up, and the table unlocks on the
    page.
  - Ads: 4 images (feed and story size for both ads), with ad text and tagged
    links in the README.
  - Tracking: about 25 events from arrival to sign-up, cookieless so no consent
    banner is needed, plus a funnel dashboard at /api/stats. Google Analytics
    and the Meta Pixel switch on by adding their IDs.

  Tested: I ran both versions end to end in headless Chrome at phone width
  (390px):
  - No horizontal scrolling.
  - The calculator, filters and "show more" all work.
  - The email typo check turns sezanm.cz into seznam.cz.
  - Sign-up works: the table unlocks and the follow-up question saves.
  - All tracking events arrived, with no JavaScript errors.

  Things you should know:
  - Live data contradicted my own assumptions: VWCE's fee is now 0.14% (from
    July 2026), SPLG has been renamed SPYM, and VTI now tracks a Morningstar
    index. That's why everything comes from a script instead of hardcoded
    numbers.
  - The Czech 40-million-CZK cap on the tax exemption was abolished from 2026.
    The cheat sheet reflects that.
  - Saving leads permanently needs a free Upstash database connected in Vercel,
    which takes a few clicks. Until then leads only appear in Vercel's logs,
    which are kept for about an hour. That's step 1 of the launch checklist.
  - Before running real ads, someone has to fill in the operator's name and
    company ID, connect the email service, and get the privacy text reviewed.
    The 4 follow-up emails are outlined but not written.

  You can see it locally right now at http://localhost:3000 (add
  /?utm_content=twins for Ad B).

✻ Sautéed for 2m 6s · done Friday 1:55 PM

> okay man, on the first look it does seem to target the younger people,        
  nothing wrong with that we can modify that later, but not i would like to     
  talk about the code, i can barely understand what is where, and it does not   
  look any framework i ever seen, so is there a better way ? i would like to    
  use Vue, and i dont know what else, there may be some usage of Astra, but     
  this seems rather simple and difficutl to follow with todays options          

● Agreed. The current code is plain JavaScript that edits the page by hand: one
  400-line app.js does everything, HTML is built from strings, and the two ad
  versions are switched with CSS tricks. That's fast, but hard to follow.

  I'm assuming "Astra" means Astro (Astra is a WordPress theme). Astro plus Vue
  is exactly what I'd recommend.

  Recommendation: Astro + Vue + TypeScript

  A landing page is about 80% static text and 20% interactive. Astro turns the
  static parts into plain HTML with no JavaScript. It loads Vue only for the
  interactive parts (calculator, table, form). You get readable components and
  keep the fast first screen that mobile ad traffic needs.

  src/
    pages/
      index.astro        ← Ad A page: lists sections in order, readable top to 
  bottom
      twins.astro        ← Ad B page: same sections, different order
      api/lead.ts        ← sign-up endpoint (save, forward, email)
      api/track.ts       ← tracking endpoint
      api/stats.ts       ← funnel dashboard
    components/
      HeroTwins.astro  Inside.astro  Faq.astro  Footer.astro   ← static, no JS
      Calculator.vue  EtfTable.vue  LeadForm.vue  ThankYou.vue  StickyCta.vue  ←
  Vue
    lib/
      calc.ts            ← the fee formula
      emailTypo.ts       ← the "seznam.cz?" check
      tracking.ts        ← one track() function used everywhere
      leadStore.ts       ← shared "has signed up" state, so the table unlocks 
  when a form submits
    data/etfs.json       ← read at build time, so the table is in the HTML (no 
  loading spinner)
  scripts/               ← data fetch + PDF/ad rendering (unchanged)
  tests/                 ← Vitest for calc/typo logic, Playwright for the full 
  signup flow

  What gets clearer:
  - Ad versions: each ad gets its own page with a readable section order. Vercel
    routes ?utm_content=twins… to the twins page. The CSS ordering tricks and
    the inline script go away.
  - Interactive parts: each is one .vue file with its HTML, logic and styles
    together. No more querySelector and string templates.
  - API: becomes ordinary Astro endpoints, with a standard Vercel setup instead
    of a hand-written dev server.
  - Testing: the end-to-end test I ran today becomes a real test in the repo,
    runnable with npm test.

  The alternative: Nuxt

  Nuxt is Vue everywhere, with built-in server routes, so there's one mental
  model if your team lives in Vue. The cost is that the whole page loads Vue
  (roughly 50–60 KB more JS) and the first screen shows up later on a slow
  phone. For an app I'd pick Nuxt; for an ad landing page, Astro.

  I'd skip a plain Vue app built with Vite: until the JavaScript loads, the
  first screen would be blank.

  Two choices for you

  1. Astro + Vue (my recommendation) or Nuxt?
  2. Styling: Tailwind, or styles inside each component? I'd keep styles in the
     components plus one shared colour/font file. This design is custom enough
     that Tailwind classes would get long. If your team already uses Tailwind,
     I'll switch.

  The look, copy, data, tracking events and README strategy stay the same; only
  the code is restructured. On the audience: noted, we'll revisit the copy and
  design for older visitors later.

  Shall I go ahead with Astro + Vue?

✻ Worked for 32s · done Friday 2:00 PM

> so you are saying that Nuxt with its server side render, for fast load, would 
  be a good way to go, maybe put the Astro + Vue vs Nuxt in a grid              

● Not quite. I said the opposite, and part of my earlier answer was imprecise,
  so here's the correction.

  With server rendering (or pre-rendering at build time), Nuxt also delivers 
  ready HTML, so the first screen appears just as fast as with Astro. My line
  "the first screen shows up later" was wrong. The real difference comes after
  the HTML arrives: how much JavaScript the phone downloads and runs before the
  calculator and form respond.

  First screen visible
  Astro + Vue: Fast: plain HTML
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Fast: plain HTML (with SSR/SSG)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  JavaScript shipped
  Astro + Vue: Only for the interactive parts (calculator, table, form). Static
  sections ship none.
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Vue + router + Nuxt runtime, roughly 20–40 KB more

  (gzipped)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Hydration (making the page interactive)
  Astro + Vue: Per component. Below-the-fold parts can wait until scrolled into
  view.
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Whole page at once on load
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Time until calculator/form respond on a mid-range phone on 4G
  Astro + Vue: A bit faster
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): A bit slower, roughly 0.1–0.3 s (my estimate, not
  measured)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Writing components
  Astro + Vue: .astro for static parts, .vue for interactive. Two syntaxes.
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): .vue everywhere. One syntax.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Sharing state between parts (e.g. sign-up unlocks the table)
  Astro + Vue: Islands are separate Vue apps, so it needs a small shared store
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Normal Vue state; just works
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Server API (lead, track, stats)
  Astro + Vue: Astro endpoints, fine for a few routes
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Nitro server routes: more complete (middleware,
  utils, caching)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Two ad variants
  Astro + Vue: Two static pages + a Vercel routing rule
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Same, or one page reading the URL on the server
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Deploy to Vercel
  Astro + Vue: Official adapter, zero config
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Zero config (auto-detected)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  If the project grows (more pages, user accounts, admin, logged-in dashboard)
  Astro + Vue: Gets awkward; Astro is built for content sites
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Built for this
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  For a Vue developer
  Astro + Vue: Learn Astro's basics (small)
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): Feels like home
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Best fit
  Astro + Vue: A pure landing page where every 100 ms counts
  Nuxt (pre-rendered or SSR): A Vue team, or a landing page that will grow into
  a
  product

  My take: for this page as it is, Astro wins on speed, but only by a small
  margin. If your team works in Vue, or this is likely to grow (more landing
  pages, a results page, a client area), Nuxt is a perfectly good choice: one
  syntax, simpler shared state, a stronger server side. The performance cost is
  small, and we can keep it small by pre-rendering both variants and keeping the
  JS light.

  So it comes down to which matters more to you, the last ~0.2 s or one Vue-only
  codebase. Which way do you want to go?

● The site is now rebuilt on Astro + Vue + TypeScript and live at
  https://etf-tahak-landing.vercel.app. Design, copy, data, tracking and
  strategy are unchanged. It's pushed to GitHub, and the automatic checks pass
  there too.

  One URL change you need to know about: each ad now has its own page instead of
  one page switching by link parameter.
  - Ad A (fees): / (https://etf-tahak-landing.vercel.app)
  - Ad B (twins): /dvojcata (https://etf-tahak-landing.vercel.app/dvojcata)

  The README has the full ad links.

  How the code is organised now

  src/pages/index.astro        ← "/" (Ad A): the section order, top to bottom
  src/pages/dvojcata.astro     ← "/dvojcata" (Ad B): same sections, different 
  order
  src/pages/api/               ← lead.ts, track.ts, stats.ts (server endpoints)
  src/components/sections/     ← each page section, one file each, plain HTML
  src/components/*.vue         ← interactive parts: Calculator, EtfTable, 
  LeadForm, ThankYou, StickyCta
  src/lib/                     ← plain logic: fee formula, email check, tracking
  src/stores/                  ← state shared between the Vue parts (e.g. 
  sign-up unlocks the table)
  The best place to start reading is src/pages/index.astro. README §12 has the
  full tour.

  Settings: everything you need for launch (Meta Pixel ID, company name and IČO,
  database, email service) is now an environment variable set in Vercel. You no
  longer edit any code to launch.

  Tests

  - Unit tests (19): the calculator maths and the email check.
  - End-to-end tests (4), on a phone-sized screen: both ads, sign-up, the table
    unlocking, returning visitors, the sign-up endpoint, and no JavaScript or
    hydration errors.
  - They run on every push to GitHub, and I also ran them against the live site.

  Problems found and fixed during the rebuild

  - The live site's sign-up was broken after the first Astro deploy. Every
    server endpoint crashed because the Vercel adapter for Astro released
    yesterday (11.0.13) packs build-only tools into the server code. I pinned
    the previous version (11.0.12) and added a check that fails the build if
    this happens again. Don't upgrade that package without re-checking.
  - Email typo check: gmail.cz was being "corrected" to email.cz. It now fixes
    the ending first, so it becomes gmail.com. A unit test caught this.
  - Hydration warning: signing up in the Ad B hero before the table below had
    loaded caused Vue warnings. Fixed.
  - Security warning: one dependency had a known high-severity issue. I pinned
    the patched version; npm now reports 0 vulnerabilities.

  The trade-off

  The page now ships about 43 KB of compressed JavaScript instead of about 10
  KB; roughly 30 KB of that is Vue itself. The first screen is still ready-made
  HTML, so it appears just as fast. Only the parts that need it become
  interactive slightly later.

  Still on your side

  - Saving leads: add the free Upstash database in Vercel. Until then, sign-ups
    only reach Vercel's logs.
  - Auto-deploy: connect the GitHub repo to the Vercel project so pushes deploy
    automatically.
  - Launch settings: fill in the environment variables listed in the README
    checklist.

● The four design directions are on a design canvas: ⧉
  https://claude.ai/artifact/3GXhywvDNGZRjRiu4egpSP

  Each direction has two phone screens, the Ad A (fees) first screen and the Ad
  B (twins) first screen, using the same copy and real numbers as the live site.
  On the fees screens the calculator works: press Play and tap the amounts and
  years. Everything that moves stops for people whose phone is set to reduce
  motion.

  A · Burza naživo
  Look: Dark trading-app look, lime and coral
  What moves: Scrolling ticker of the ETF pairs at the top, pulsing "live" dot,
  blinking cursor on the loss
  Who it suits: 22–40, already use trading apps. Could feel "crypto" to cautious

  investors.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  B · Pop-art komiks
  Look: Cobalt blue, yellow and tomato red, comic dots and stickers
  What moves: Wobbling starburst with the loss, stickers popping in, a "broker
  says no" speech bubble
  Who it suits: Most attention-grabbing in a feed. Least serious, so the copy
  has
  to stay very factual.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  C · Bankovka
  Look: Banknote style: guilloche pattern, serif numbers, gold strip, red stamp
  What moves: Shimmering gold strip, slowly rotating rosette, stamps that land
  on
  the twins
  Who it suits: The widest audience, including 35–55, which answers your point
  that the current design skews young.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  D · Chat
  Look: The page is a WhatsApp-style conversation
  What moves: Messages slide in, a typing indicator, your answers appear as your

  own messages
  Who it suits: Everyone on mobile. The email field is the chat box, so signing
  up feels like replying. Less room for the comparison table.

  My pick: C or D. C is the safest step up, distinctive and trustworthy. D is
  the bolder bet on conversions, because entering the email feels like answering
  a message. A or B work if you're deliberately going after a young,
  social-first audience. Sticky notes beside each row on the canvas explain each
  direction.

  You can mix: for example C's look on Ad A and D's chat flow on Ad B, or test
  two directions against each other.

  The canvas is private; share it from its Share menu if anyone else should see
  it. Pick a direction (or a mix) and I'll build it into the Astro site.

● Both widths now correctly show −9 384 000 Kč on one line, with bar values

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Fixed and deployed: the loss number now always stays on one line, and its font
  shrinks only when the number gets long. All 9 end-to-end tests pass against
  the live site.

  The bug: the longest result, −9 384 000 Kč (20 000 Kč a month, 30 years, fee
  slider at 3 %), pushed "Kč" onto a second line on every screen size, desktop
  included, because the card is limited to the column width. The two amounts
  next to the bars (up to 23 090 000 Kč) could wrap the same way.

  The fix (src/components/Calculator.vue):
  - The line can no longer break: the number is set never to wrap, whatever its
    length.
  - The font size is calculated: every character in the number font is exactly
    0.6× the font size wide. The size is the width of the card's result area
    divided by the number of characters × 0.6, and never bigger than the design
    size. So "−466 000 Kč" stays at full size, and only longer numbers get
    smaller.
  - Older browsers: phones from before 2022 can't do that calculation. They get
    a fixed size that fits the longest value even on a 320px screen.
  - Bar amounts: they also stay on one line now.

  Test: tests/e2e/calculator-fit.spec.ts sets the worst case at 320, 360, 390,
  430 and 1280px. It checks that both the loss number and the bar amounts sit on
  a single line inside the card. I ran it against the old code first, and it
  failed at all five widths ("wraps onto 2 lines"), so it does catch this bug.
  It now runs on every push.

● I've put three versions of the effect on a new page of the design canvas,
  "Email & unlock glow", which opens first: ⧉
  https://claude.ai/artifact/3GXhywvDNGZRjRiu4egpSP

  Each is a phone screen with the calculator result, the email form and three
  table cards (one open twin, two locked). Press Play and type an email to watch
  the effect move from the field to the button once the address is valid. The
  Tweaks switch on each screen flips between empty and filled without typing.

  1 · Obíhající linka (running border)
  Empty email field: A lime light runs around the field's border
  Valid email entered: The field turns solid lime with a ✓, and the running
  light
  jumps to the button, which gets a soft glow
  🔒 Odemknout buttons: The same running light, each button out of step with the

  others
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  2 · Dýchající záře (breathing glow)
  Empty email field: A soft lime glow slowly pulses around the field
  Valid email entered: The field turns solid lime with a ✓; the button glows
  stronger and gently pulses
  🔒 Odemknout buttons: A slow pulsing glow, out of step
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  3 · Skener (light sweep)
  Empty email field: A scanning line runs along the bottom of the field, like a
  terminal
  Valid email entered: The field turns solid lime with a ✓; a light sweep passes

  over the button every few seconds and the arrow nudges forward
  🔒 Odemknout buttons: The same sweep, out of step

  My pick: 1, since it's exactly the running border you described. A possible
  refinement if the table feels busy: running border on the email field and
  button, but the calmer breathing glow (2) on the Odemknout buttons. On a phone
  only two or three locked buttons are on screen at once, and I'd pause the
  effect on buttons that are scrolled out of view.

  All three switch off for people who have turned on reduced motion on their
  phone. Tell me which one, or a mix, and I'll build it into the site.

※ recap: We're building the ETF lead-capture landing page; the running-border email field and light-sweep buttons are now live and tested. Next, connect the free Upstash database in Vercel so real leads get saved before any ads run.

> is there a GDPR consent for the emial ?                                       

● No, there isn't a consent checkbox, and I'd fix that before running ads.

  How it works now:
  - The form only asks for the email. Under the button it says what happens
    ("PDF hned… Žádné telefonáty, žádný poradce") and links to the privacy page.
  - The privacy page says the email is used to send the requested cheat sheet
    and "4 follow-up emails that are part of it", and that further marketing
    goes out only with consent.
  - The legal basis is that the visitor asked for it (GDPR art. 6(1)(b)). That's
    fine for delivering the PDF.

  The gap is the 4 follow-up emails:
  - Czech law: the Czech act on information society services (Act 480/2004 Sb.,
    §7) requires prior consent for commercial emails. The "existing customer"
    exception covers contacts gained when selling something, and a free download
    most likely doesn't qualify.
  - GDPR: consent can't be made a condition of getting the freebie (art. 7(4)).
  - In practice: if those emails steer people toward a broker, the operator's
    services or affiliate links, they count as commercial communication and need
    explicit, separate consent. Calling them "part of the cheat sheet" is a
    grey zone the Czech data protection office (ÚOOÚ) tends to reject.
  - Nothing records consent: there's currently no record of when someone agreed,
    or to which wording.

  My recommendation: ask for consent after sign-up, on the thank-you panel
  1. Before submit, nothing changes. It stays one field with no checkbox, so
     conversion isn't hurt, and the PDF goes out on the "you asked for it"
     basis.
  2. On the thank-you panel, add a clear one-tap opt-in with its own button, for
     example "Chci i 4 krátké e-maily s tipy (broker, trvalý příkaz, daně, 
     DIP). Odhlásit se můžu kdykoli." Never pre-ticked.
  3. Store proof with the lead: a consent yes/no flag, a timestamp, the exact
     wording shown (with a version number) and the form location. The webhook
     passes it on to your email tool, so only people who consented get the
     sequence.
  4. Update the copy: the FAQ and privacy page wording become "PDF now; tips
     only if you want them", and the README checklist gets updated too.
  5. Track the opt-in: it gets its own tracking event and a column in the funnel
     dashboard, so you can see the opt-in rate.

  Alternatives:
  - Unticked checkbox in the form: legally the most standard, but it adds
    friction at the most sensitive moment.
  - Double opt-in: a confirmation email before the sequence starts. It gives the
    strongest proof, but loses a meaningful share of people before they
    confirm.

  I'm not a lawyer, so the final wording should go past whoever handles the
  operator's legal side. But option 1 is the common pattern and keeps the form
  at one field.

  Shall I implement the thank-you opt-in?

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● I've looked into PostHog (pricing, EU hosting, cookieless mode, Astro and
  Vercel setup). Short version: it fits this project well, as long as it runs in
  cookieless mode in the EU. The site already has a stub for it, but only
  behind the cookie banner; I'd change that.

  What PostHog gives us

  - Funnels from our existing events: lp_view → calc_interact → form_start → 
    lead_submitted → optin_given is clickable, broken down by ad creative
    (utm_content), page variant and A/B arm, with no SQL. That's the "track the
    whole journey" part of the brief, done properly instead of my Redis
    dashboard.
  - Web analytics dashboard: traffic sources, pages, devices and bounce, out of
    the box.
  - Free tier: about 1M events, 5k session recordings and 1M feature-flag
    requests per month (pricing overview
    (https://costbench.com/software/product-analytics/posthog/free-plan/),
    usercall (https://usercall.co/post/posthog-pricing)). My rough traffic
    estimate is about 200k events a month at 2–3k CZK/day of ads, so we'd stay
    free.
  - EU Cloud (Frankfurt), which keeps the Czech/GDPR story simple.

  The important part: cookieless mode (PostHog docs 
  (https://posthog.com/docs/data/cookieless-tracking))

  - How it works: with cookieless_mode: "always", PostHog stores nothing in the
    browser. It counts visitors with a server-side hash of the project, a daily 
    rotating salt, the IP address, the browser's user-agent string and the
    hostname. The salt is deleted after the day's events are processed, and
    PostHog says the hash itself isn't personal data.
  - Result: tracking from the very first ad click without a cookie banner. That
    matters because a banner on a mobile ad landing page costs conversions.
  - Trade-offs:
    - No session replay and no surveys without consent, since both need browser
      storage.
    - No geo-location or bot detection.
    - Returning visitors look new each day (the salt rotates). That's fine for a
      one-visit ad funnel.
    - No identify(). We wouldn't want to send emails to analytics anyway.
  - One switch is needed: "Cookieless server hash mode" has to be turned on in
    the PostHog project settings (Web analytics), otherwise the events are
    dropped.
  - Why not the other mode: on_reject mode would need the banner, and captures
    nothing until the visitor clicks it. That's worse for our funnel.

  Alternatives, briefly

  ┌────────────────┬──────────┬───────────┬───────────┬────────────────────┐
  │                │ Funnels  │  Cookie   │ Session   │                    │
  │                │  by ad   │  banner   │  replay   │      Verdict       │
  │                │          │  needed   │           │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ PostHog (EU,   │ ✅       │ No        │ Only with │ Best fit           │
  │ cookieless)    │          │           │  consent  │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Our Redis      │ Basic    │           │           │ Keep as a backup   │
  │ funnel         │ table    │ No        │ ✗         │ to cross-check     │
  │ (current)      │          │           │           │                    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │                │          │           │           │ Banner hurts       │
  │ GA4            │ ✅       │ Yes (CZ)  │ ✗         │ conversion; data   │
  │                │          │           │           │ goes to Google     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Plausible /    │          │           │           │ Simpler, but       │
  │ Umami          │ Limited  │ No        │ ✗         │ weaker on custom   │
  │                │          │           │           │ funnels            │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Microsoft      │ ✗        │ Yes       │ ✅        │ Free replays, but  │
  │ Clarity        │          │           │           │ needs consent      │
  └────────────────┴──────────┴───────────┴───────────┴────────────────────┘

  The Meta Pixel stays as it is, behind the consent banner. It's for ad
  optimisation, not analytics, and PostHog doesn't replace it.

  Proposed implementation

  1. Load it properly: use the posthog-js npm package (v1.438), loaded lazily
     after the page is interactive, so the first screen stays fast. Settings:
     cookieless_mode: "always", EU host, defaults: "2026-05-30".
  2. Get past ad blockers: route PostHog through our own domain with Vercel
     rewrites to eu.i.posthog.com / eu-assets.i.posthog.com under a non-obvious
     path (Vercel proxy guide (https://posthog.com/docs/advanced/proxy/vercel)).
     Ad blockers block posthog.com but not our own domain, so we'd lose fewer
     visitors from the data.
  3. Send all existing ~25 events with ad context attached (variant, utm_*, ask,
     loc), so every chart can be split by creative. No code changes are needed
     in the components, since everything already goes through track().
  4. Keep our own /api/track funnel as a backup. Comparing its counts with
     PostHog's shows how much ad blockers hide.
  5. Session replay only after consent: if the cookie banner is ever shown (when
     the Meta Pixel is on), "Souhlasím" also switches on replay. Without
     consent there's no replay.
  6. Documentation: a README section explaining the setup and listing which
     funnels and insights to create in PostHog, plus privacy page wording
     ("cookieless analytics, PostHog EU").
  7. Tests: an end-to-end check that events reach the proxy path and that
     nothing is written to cookies or local storage.

  What I need from you: create a PostHog EU Cloud project, turn on "Cookieless
  server hash mode", and give me the project token (phc_…; it's public, it goes
  into an environment variable). I can build everything before you have the
  token, and it stays off until the token is set.

  Shall I go ahead?

● PostHog is built in and deployed, but it stays off until you set a project 
  token. Everything else is tested: 12 end-to-end tests pass locally and on
  GitHub CI, and against the live site the 10 non-PostHog tests pass while the 2
  PostHog tests are skipped until there's a token.

  To switch it on (about 10 minutes):
  1. Create a project in PostHog EU Cloud (eu.posthog.com).
  2. In Project settings → Web analytics, turn on "Cookieless server hash mode".
     Without it, PostHog drops every event.
  3. Put the project token (phc_…; it's public) into Vercel as
     PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY and redeploy. Or send it to me and I'll do it.

  How it works:
  - Every existing event (visit, calculator, table, form, sign-up, consent,
    sharing…) goes to PostHog, tagged with the page version, the ad parameters
    (utm_*) and the A/B arm. Funnels split by ad need no extra code.
  - Cookieless: nothing is saved on the visitor's device. PostHog counts visits
    with an anonymous fingerprint whose secret changes daily, so no cookie
    banner is needed. Emails never go to PostHog. A test confirms nothing
    PostHog-related ends up in cookies or browser storage.
  - Through our own domain: requests go to /rq7/… on the site and Vercel
    forwards them to PostHog's EU servers. That way ad blockers that block
    posthog.com don't erase visitors. I checked on the live site that this
    forwarding works.
  - Fast: PostHog loads only after the page is interactive, so the first screen
    doesn't wait for it. Events from before that are queued and sent with their
    original times. I used PostHog's "slim" version: about 50 KB instead of 100
    KB.
  - Session replay only for visitors who accept cookies, and the banner only
    exists once Google Analytics or the Meta Pixel is set up.
  - Our own funnel stays as a backup. Comparing it with PostHog shows how many
    visitors ad blockers still hide.

  Two things to know:
  - The slim version is labelled experimental by PostHog. Our tests cover
    everything we rely on: events go through /rq7, they carry the ad tags,
    nothing is stored on the device. If it ever misbehaves, switching to the
    standard 100 KB version is a one-line change.
  - PostHog ignores automated browsers. It silently drops events from them, so
    our test browser's events vanished at first. On the live site that's what we
    want (no bot traffic in the numbers), so I only turned it off on the local
    development server. If you ever test with an automated browser on the live
    site and see nothing in PostHog, that's why.

  After switching it on, README §8 lists the reports worth building in PostHog.
  The main one: visit → saw the calculator result → started typing the email →
  signed up → opted in to tips, split by ad. The privacy page also explains the
  cookieless tracking now (in Czech).

● Web Search("PostHog where to find project token phc_ project settings

  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

● Project token
  - In PostHog: Settings → General. The field is called Project token and starts
    with phc_ (PostHog docs
    (https://posthog.com/docs/health-checks/authorized-urls), project settings
    reference
    (https://www.mintlify.com/PostHog/posthog/configuration/projects)).
  - It's public and can only send events, never read your data, so it's safe in
    the browser. Paste it to me or into Vercel as PUBLIC_POSTHOG_KEY.
  - Don't use a personal API key (starts with phx_). That one can read data and
    must never go in the site.

  Authorized URLs (Project settings → Authorized URLs), add:
  https://etf-tahak-landing.vercel.app
  - Add your own domain too once you have one (e.g. https://etftahak.cz).
  - Add http://localhost:4321 if you want the PostHog toolbar while developing
    locally.
  - Just the site address: no /dvojcata and no /rq7 (that's only the hidden
    route events take to PostHog).

  This list isn't needed for collecting events; those work without it. It's what
  lets PostHog's toolbar and heatmaps open on your site, and some web-analytics
  filters use it (docs
  (https://posthog.com/docs/health-checks/authorized-urls)). Session replay has
  a separate list ("Authorized domains for recordings"). Add the same address
  there only if you later turn on replay for visitors who accept cookies.

  Don't forget "Cookieless server hash mode" under Settings → Web analytics.
  Without it, PostHog drops every event, because our site runs cookieless.

  Watch out for the export file: /export saved the whole conversation into the
  project folder (2026-10-09-164202-hey-man-….txt). The repo is public, and my
  commits stage everything with git add -A, so it would get published with the
  next commit. Move it out of the folder, or tell me and I'll add it to
  .gitignore.

● Before planning a refactor, I'll check whether any files changed since my last

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Fair point. Here's how I'd do it. Nothing has changed in the code since my
  last commit; the "modified" files were only line-ending noise.

  Plan: Tailwind CSS + Pinia

  Tailwind v4 (4.3, through its Vite plugin)
  - Design tokens move into Tailwind's theme (@theme): lime, coral, surface,
    line, fonts, radii. The markup then reads bg-surface text-lime font-mono 
    rounded-xl instead of class names that point to a style block further down.
  - Every <style> block in the components goes away; styling sits on the
    elements.
  - Repeated pieces become tiny components instead of long class strings:
    Button, Chip, Card, Eyebrow. A button is then <Button variant="primary">
    rather than 15 utilities copied everywhere. This is Tailwind's own
    recommendation, and it's what keeps it readable.
  - The few effects utilities can't express (running border, light sweep, ticker
    tape, pulsing dot, number rise-in) go into one file,
    src/styles/effects.css, as named Tailwind utilities. The markup just says
    class="comet-border" or class="sheen".

  Pinia (4.0) instead of the current nanostores
  - useLeadStore: leadId, submittedFrom, markLead(), saved in the browser so
    returning visitors are recognised.
  - useCalculatorStore: monthly amount, years and fee, plus computed result and
    loss. The calculation logic moves out of Calculator.vue into the store, so
    the component is mostly template.
  - Important for Astro: each interactive part of the page is a separate Vue
    app, so they only share state if they share one Pinia instance. I'd install
    it through Astro's appEntrypoint (src/pages/_app.ts), the documented way to
    add Vue plugins in Astro. The existing test for "signing up unlocks the
    table" proves the sharing works.

  What stays the same: the look (the goal is pixel-identical), the copy,
  tracking, PostHog, the API and the data.

  How I'd verify it:
  - Before/after screenshots of both pages plus the thank-you state at 390px,
    compared side by side.
  - The full test suite. Tests currently find a few elements by CSS class (.big,
    .field--run…); I'd switch those to data-testid, so tests no longer break
    when styling changes.

  The honest trade-off: some heavily styled elements (the calculator result, the
  ETF cards) will still carry long class lists. Extracting the small components
  keeps that manageable, but it's a different readability, not zero styling
  noise.

  Your files

  Two untracked items appeared in the repo: ai-log/ and docs/REDME.md (typo for
  README?). Should they go into the public repo? Until you say so, I'll leave
  them out of every commit.

  Shall I go ahead with the refactor?

           tatic parts) and loads [Vue](https://vuejs.org) only for the i
           nteractive "islands": calculator, table, form, sticky bar. API

           hole page hydrates; Astro keeps the JS to what's interactive.)

       95  ## Fifth iteration: PostHog
       96  
       97  PostHog EU Cloud in cookieless mode (no banner, daily-rotating
            server-side hash, nothing on the device), proxied through our
            own domain (`/rq7`), loaded after the page is interactive usi
           ng the slim build. All existing `track()` events flow into it 
           with the ad context attached, so funnels and breakdowns by cre
           ative need no extra code. The own Redis funnel stays as a back
           up and as a way to measure how much ad blockers hide. Session 
           replay only after cookie consent. Setup and the insights to cr
           eate are in README §8.

  Tailwind + Pinia refactor

  - Styling: every component's separate CSS block is gone; styling now sits on
    the elements as Tailwind classes.
    - Colours: only the brand colours exist (bg-surface, text-lime,
      text-coral…); Tailwind's default palette is switched off.
    - Building blocks: repeated pieces are small components (Btn, Chip, Section,
      Heading, Eyebrow, Lede), so markup reads <Btn variant="primary"> instead
      of long copied class lists.
    - Animations: the running border, light sweep, ticker and live dot are named
      utilities in one file, src/styles/effects.css; the markup just says
      class="sheen".
  - State: two Pinia stores, useLeadStore (signed up?) and useCalculatorStore
    (inputs and result, with the maths moved out of the component). One Pinia
    instance is shared by all Vue parts of the page through src/vue-app.ts.

  How I verified "same visuals, same function":
  - Before changing anything, I recorded pixel screenshots of 13 page states on
    the old code: both pages at phone and desktop width, the privacy page,
    calculator worst case, form errors and typo hint, sorted and filtered table,
    open FAQ, thank-you with opt-in, returning visitor, and the sticky bar.
  - After the refactor, all 13 match, in 3 runs out of 3. The comparison caught
    two real 1–2 px differences, which I fixed: list spacing that came from
    browser defaults, and one padding value.
  - Tests: all 12 end-to-end tests pass locally and on GitHub CI, and the 10
    that run against the live site pass there too. Tests now find elements by
    data-testid or role, so future styling changes won't break them.
  - Visual check you can rerun: npm run test:visual (-- -u to accept an intended
    change). It's local only, because screenshots depend on the operating
    system's fonts.
  - Trade-off: the page's JavaScript grew from ~45 KB to ~53 KB gzipped, from
    Pinia plus the class names now living in components.

  PostHog: why you see no numbers

  What I checked from outside:
  - Key and region are right: the key is valid on PostHog's EU servers, which
    the site sends to, and rejected by the US ones.
  - The site sends events: just now, a real visit (utm_source = claude_check2)
    sent 5 batches through our domain, all accepted by PostHog, with nothing
    stored in the browser.

  PostHog accepts events and only filters them afterwards, so the cause is in
  your project. In order of likelihood:
  1. "Cookieless server hash mode" is off. Settings → Web analytics. Our events
     have no cookies, and without this switch PostHog silently discards all of
     them. This is my best guess.
  2. You're looking at Web analytics instead of raw events. Open Activity
     (Explore events) for the last 24 hours and look for $pageview, lp_view and
     calc_change from my two visits (claude_check, claude_check2).
  3. The "Filter out internal and test users" toggle at the top of the insights
     hides the visits.
  4. A different project: check that Settings → General shows the token starting
     with phc_B5ms.

  Also keep in mind that no ads are running yet, so the only real visits so far
  are mine and yours. Our automated tests deliberately never send anything to
  PostHog.

  Tell me if they should go into the repo.
